Manifestations\n- S049 (Eating_Large_Amount): On these nights I’ll order enough delivery for three or four people—like a family-sized pasta, a big pizza, dessert, and sides—and I just keep picking at everything until the containers are basically scraped clean, even though it’s just me in the apartment.\n- S050 (Loss_of_Control): Once I get home from these long launch days and start eating in front \n- S052 (Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full): I keep going past the point where my stomach feels tight and stretched, to where standing up to go to the bathroom is uncomfortable, but I’ll still take a few more bites even though I feel slightly sick. \n- S055 (Negative_Affect_After_Binge): Afterwards I just sit there among all the empty containers, feeling disgusted and furious with myself for doing it again, and I usually end up scrolling through my work emails or Instagram to avoid thinking about how ashamed I am to be hiding this from everyone.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- When I get home after these long launch days, a switch just flips and I go on autopilot to cope with the constant pressure and tight deadlines. While I used to truly love food, it has unfortunately become an unhealthy outlet for stress rather than something I actually enjoy.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there are times when you eat in response to emotional states, such as feeling anxious, bored, or upset, outside of the evening routine after work?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed this happening outside of my evening routine. It seems almost entirely tied to winding down at home after those long, stressful workdays, rather than happening at other times of the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant weight gain or any other physical health issues as a result of these frequent episodes of overeating?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about significant weight gain because I haven't checked the scale lately. But physically, I feel completely wiped out and extremely uncomfortable after these episodes, which makes it really hard to get through the next morning.\n\nDoctor: Can you clarify if you experience a sense of loss of control during these episodes, where you feel like you cannot stop eating even if you want to, and how does this feeling relate to the frequency of these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes, I absolutely feel that loss of control—it's like a switch flips and I'm on autopilot, telling myself to stop but still walking back to the kitchen for more. This happens at least a couple of times every week, usually on those late nights when I get home from the office completely exhausted.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe the exact moment when you feel like you're losing control over your eating, is it when you start eating, after a certain amount of food, or when you're feeling a specific emotion?\n\nPatient: It starts almost as soon as I get home from a long launch day and sit down in front of my laptop with the food. That is when the switch flips and I go on autopilot, telling myself to stop but continuing to walk back to the kitchen for more anyway.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there are any specific thoughts, feelings, or physical sensations that you experience right before you start eating uncontrollably, which might help differentiate between your emotional state and the act of eating itself?\n\nPatient: Right before it starts, I feel completely drained and overwhelmed by the day's pressures, with an intense urge to just shut down. Once I sit down in front of my laptop with the food, it triggers that \"autopilot\" feeling where the switch flips and I lose all control.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever experience episodes of eating in secret, hiding your eating from others due to feelings of shame or guilt about the amount or type of food you're consuming?"

Can you please tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I'm here because I feel like once I start eating at night after work, I just can’t stop or control how much I’m eating. It has gotten to the point where it's really starting to worry me, and I felt like I needed to seek some help.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe a typical episode where you feel like you've lost control over your eating, and is this behavior followed by feelings of guilt, shame, or self-criticism?\n\nPatient: On a typical night, I'll order enough takeout for three or four people—like a large pizza, pasta, and sides—and eat it incredibly fast on autopilot, barely even chewing, until my stomach is painfully full and I feel sick. Afterward, I'm left sitting among the empty containers feeling completely disgusted and ashamed of myself, so I'll scroll through work emails or social media just to distract myself from those awful feelings.\n\nDoctor: During or after these episodes of eating large amounts of food, do you ever engage in behaviors like vomiting, using laxatives, or excessive exercise to try and compensate for the calories you've consumed?\n\nPatient: No, I don't do anything like that at all. I have never tried to make myself throw up, use laxatives, or exercise excessively to make up for the food I've eaten.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of losing control over your eating happen, and have you noticed any pattern or regularity to when they occur?\n\nPatient: These episodes have been happening at least a couple of times every week for about the last three months. They almost always occur on the evenings when I get home late from the office, especially since the intense product launch campaign ramped up at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that the stress or emotional state you're in, especially related to work, contributes to your loss of control over eating, or is it more about the food itself or the evening routine?\n\nPatient: It is absolutely tied to the intense work stress and emotional exhaustion from my job. While I used to truly love food, it has unfortunately become an unhealthy outlet for stress rather than something I actually enjoy.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there are times when you eat in response to emotional states, such as feeling anxious, bored, or upset, outside of the evening routine after work?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed this happening outside of my evening routine. It seems almost entirely tied to winding down at home after those long, stressful workdays, rather than happening at other times of the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant weight gain or any other physical health issues as a result of these frequent episodes of overeating?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about significant weight gain because I haven't checked the scale lately. But physically, I feel completely wiped out and extremely uncomfortable after these episodes, which makes it really hard to get through the next morning.\n\nDoctor: Can you clarify if you experience a sense of loss of control during these episodes, where you feel like you cannot stop eating even if you want to, and how does this feeling relate to the frequency of these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes, I absolutely feel that loss of control—it's like a switch flips and I'm on autopilot, telling myself to stop but still walking back to the kitchen for more. This happens at least a couple of times every week, usually on those late nights when I get home from the office completely exhausted.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe the exact moment when you feel like you're losing control over your eating, is it when you start eating, after a certain amount of food, or when you're feeling a specific emotion?\n\nPatient: It starts almost as soon as I get home from a long launch day and sit down in front of my laptop with the food. That is when the switch flips and I go on autopilot, telling myself to stop but continuing to walk back to the kitchen for more anyway.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there are any specific thoughts, feelings, or physical sensations that you experience right before you start eating uncontrollably, which might help differentiate between your emotional state and the act of eating itself?\n\nPatient: Right before it starts, I feel completely drained and overwhelmed by the day's pressures, with an intense urge to just shut down. Once I sit down in front of my laptop with the food, it triggers that \"autopilot\" feeling where the switch flips and I lose all control.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever experience episodes of eating in secret, hiding your eating from others due to feelings of shame or guilt about the amount or type of food you're consuming?\n\nPatient: Yes, I only ever do this in secret at my apartment because I feel so deeply ashamed and hide it from everyone. It has gotten to the point where I've started turning down invites to my yoga group and our monthly dinner parties because I feel too embarrassed and disgusted with myself to be around people.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you have ever experienced episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short period, but without feeling the emotional distress or loss of control that you described, perhaps during celebrations or special occasions?\n\nPatient: I'm not quite sure, and I haven't really thought about it that way. My focus has been entirely on these recent distressing and out-of-control episodes after work, so I haven't really paid attention to anything else."

I have never tried to make myself throw up, use laxatives, or exercise excessively to make up for the food I've eaten.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of losing control over your eating happen, and have you noticed any pattern or regularity to when they occur?\n\nPatient: These episodes have been happening at least a couple of times every week for about the last three months. They almost always occur on the evenings when I get home late from the office, especially since the intense product launch campaign ramped up at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that the stress or emotional state you're in, especially related to work, contributes to your loss of control over eating, or is it more about the food itself or the evening routine?\n\nPatient: It is absolutely tied to the intense work stress and emotional exhaustion from my job. When I get home after these long launch days, a switch just flips and I go on autopilot to cope with the constant pressure and tight deadlines. While I used to truly love food, it has unfortunately become an unhealthy outlet for stress rather than something I actually enjoy.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there are times when you eat in response to emotional states, such as feeling anxious, bored, or upset, outside of the evening routine after work?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed this happening outside of my evening routine. It seems almost entirely tied to winding down at home after those long, stressful workdays, rather than happening at other times of the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant weight gain or any other physical health issues as a result of these frequent episodes of overeating?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about significant weight gain because I haven't checked the scale lately. But physically, I feel completely wiped out and extremely uncomfortable after these episodes, which makes it really hard to get through the next morning.\n\nDoctor: Can you clarify if you experience a sense of loss of control during these episodes, where you feel like you cannot stop eating even if you want to, and how does this feeling relate to the frequency of these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes, I absolutely feel that loss of control—it's like a switch flips and I'm on autopilot, telling myself to stop but still walking back to the kitchen for more. This happens at least a couple of times every week, usually on those late nights when I get home from the office completely exhausted.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe the exact moment when you feel like you're losing control over your eating, is it when you start eating, after a certain amount of food, or when you're feeling a specific emotion?\n\nPatient: It starts almost as soon as I get home from a long launch day and sit down in front of my laptop with the food. That is when the switch flips and I go on autopilot, telling myself to stop but continuing to walk back to the kitchen for more anyway.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there are any specific thoughts, feelings, or physical sensations that you experience right before you start eating uncontrollably, which might help differentiate between your emotional state and the act of eating itself?\n\nPatient: Right before it starts, I feel completely drained and overwhelmed by the day's pressures, with an intense urge to just shut down. Once I sit down in front of my laptop with the food, it triggers that \"autopilot\" feeling where the switch flips and I lose all control.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever experience episodes of eating in secret, hiding your eating from others due to feelings of shame or guilt about the amount or type of food you're consuming?\n\nPatient: Yes, I only ever do this in secret at my apartment because I feel so deeply ashamed and hide it from everyone. It has gotten to the point where I've started turning down invites to my yoga group and our monthly dinner parties because I feel too embarrassed and disgusted with myself to be around people.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you have ever experienced episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short period, but without feeling the emotional distress or loss of control that you described, perhaps during celebrations or special occasions?\n\nPatient: I'm not quite sure, and I haven't really thought about it that way. My focus has been entirely on these recent distressing and out-of-control episodes after work, so I haven't really paid attention to anything else.\n\nDoctor: Can you clarify if, during these episodes of eating large amounts of food, you experience any pleasure or enjoyment from the taste, smell, or texture of the food, or is it purely a mechanical act to cope with stress and emotional exhaustion?\n\nPatient: No, there is absolutely no pleasure or enjoyment in it. I'm just eating on autopilot, shoveling the food in so fast that I barely even taste it. It feels entirely driven and out of control, rather than about actually savoring the food."

However, the absence of compensatory behaviors such as vomiting, laxatives, or excessive exercise, and the primary trigger being emotional stress, especially in the evening after work, suggests that another condition related to eating disorders could also be considered. The patient's secret eating due to shame, lack of pleasure during the episodes, and the pattern of eating as a response to emotional states, particularly stress, supports the inclusion of another specific eating disorder in the differential diagnosis. The previous candidate remains a possibility due to the patient's reported loss of control and emotional distress, but the additional information gathered suggests considering another condition that is characterized by recurrent episodes of eating large amounts of food in a short period, accompanied by feelings of lack of control, and significant distress, but without the regular use of compensatory measures. Afterward, I'm left sitting among the empty containers feeling completely disgusted and ashamed of myself, so I'll scroll through work emails or social media just to distract myself from those awful feelings.\n\nDoctor: During or after these episodes of eating large amounts of food, do you ever engage in behaviors like vomiting, using laxatives, or excessive exercise to try and compensate for the calories you've consumed?\n\nPatient: No, I don't do anything like that at all. I have never tried to make myself throw up, use laxatives, or exercise excessively to make up for the food I've eaten.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of losing control over your eating happen, and have you noticed any pattern or regularity to when they occur?\n\nPatient: These episodes have been happening at least a couple of times every week for about the last three months. They almost always occur on the evenings when I get home late from the office, especially since the intense product launch campaign ramped up at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that the stress or emotional state you're in, especially related to work, contributes to your loss of control over eating, or is it more about the food itself or the evening routine?\n\nPatient: It is absolutely tied to the intense work stress and emotional exhaustion from my job. When I get home after these long launch days, a switch just flips and I go on autopilot to cope with the constant pressure and tight deadlines. While I used to truly love food, it has unfortunately become an unhealthy outlet for stress rather than something I actually enjoy.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there are times when you eat in response to emotional states, such as feeling anxious, bored, or upset, outside of the evening routine after work?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed this happening outside of my evening routine. It seems almost entirely tied to winding down at home after those long, stressful workdays, rather than happening at other times of the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant weight gain or any other physical health issues as a result of these frequent episodes of overeating?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about significant weight gain because I haven't checked the scale lately. But physically, I feel completely wiped out and extremely uncomfortable after these episodes, which makes it really hard to get through the next morning.\n\nDoctor: Can you clarify if you experience a sense of loss of control during these episodes, where you feel like you cannot stop eating even if you want to, and how does this feeling relate to the frequency of these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes, I absolutely feel that loss of control—it's like a switch flips and I'm on autopilot, telling myself to stop but still walking back to the kitchen for more. This happens at least a couple of times every week, usually on those late nights when I get home from the office completely exhausted.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe the exact moment when you feel like you're losing control over your eating, is it when you start eating, after a certain amount of food, or when you're feeling a specific emotion?\n\nPatient: It starts almost as soon as I get home from a long launch day and sit down in front of my laptop with the food. That is when the switch flips and I go on autopilot, telling myself to stop but continuing to walk back to the kitchen for more anyway.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there are any specific thoughts, feelings, or physical sensations that you experience right before you start eating uncontrollably, which might help differentiate between your emotional state and the act of eating itself?\n\nPatient: Right before it starts, I feel completely drained and overwhelmed by the day's pressures, with an intense urge to just shut down. Once I sit down in front of my laptop with the food, it triggers that \"autopilot\" feeling where the switch flips and I lose all control.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever experience episodes of eating in secret, hiding your eating from others due to feelings of shame or guilt about the amount or type of food you're consuming?\n\nPatient: Yes, I only ever do this in secret at my apartment because I feel so deeply ashamed and hide it from everyone. It has gotten to the point where I've started turning down invites to my yoga group and our monthly dinner parties because I feel too embarrassed and disgusted with myself to be around people.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you have ever experienced episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short period, but without feeling the emotional distress or loss of control that you described, perhaps during celebrations or special occasions?\n\nPatient: I'm not quite sure, and I haven't really thought about it that way. My focus has been entirely on these recent distressing and out-of-control episodes after work, so I haven't really paid attention to anything else.\n\nDoctor: Can you clarify if, during these episodes of eating large amounts of food, you experience any pleasure or enjoyment from the taste, smell, or texture of the food, or is it purely a mechanical act to cope with stress and emotional exhaustion?\n\nPatient: No, there is absolutely no pleasure or enjoyment in it. I'm just eating on autopilot, shoveling the food in so fast that I barely even taste it. It feels entirely driven and out of control, rather than about actually savoring the food.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how often, during these episodes of eating, you feel like you're not enjoying the food at all, but rather just going through the motions, compared to times when you might eat a lot but feel more in control or even enjoy it?\n\nPatient: During these nighttime episodes, it is almost always the case that I'm not enjoying the food at all and am just going through the motions. It feels completely driven and devoid of actual pleasure, which is a stark contrast to how I used to love dining out and savoring food. I'm just on autopilot, barely tasting or chewing what I'm eating."
  }
]

The patient's description of eating large amounts of food in a short period, feeling a loss of control, and experiencing significant distress and guilt afterward, without any compensatory behaviors, aligns more closely with F50.8. The patient's consistent reporting of stress and emotional exhaustion as triggers, and the absence of pleasure or enjoyment during these episodes, further supports this diagnosis.", "is_final": true}

Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).
